People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

Um… I’m here because things at work have gotten kind of, I don’t know, coordinated. Dispatch and compliance keep tracking my route changes through the computer system, and then there are these little comments on the radio that line up too well. People say it’s just normal oversight, but I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me.

And it’s not just me being sensitive, because I know how the routes are supposed to work—I’ve been doing transportation a while, and you start to notice patterns, like who gets sent where, who suddenly goes quiet when you come in. Anyway, that’s why I came in.

People can show you one screen or one report, but that doesn’t mean certain drivers aren’t still reporting things. Sometimes the system looks clean on paper, you know? Like at work they’ll say everything is “procedure,” but then everyone knows there’s the real way things get handled.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: I don’t feel like singing with choir or having the softball team over anymore, and most days after my shift I just sit at the kitchen table with the planner open but can’t make myself start dinner, calls, or anything else.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve fallen behind on route reports and my instructions have gotten confusing enough that drivers complain, and at home my husband has had to take over meals, calls, and basic planning because I’m not keeping up.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been building for about 8 months, since I was put in the temporary dispatch lead role.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: The tracking through the computer system and radio comments has felt clear to me for about the past 2 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / transportation_worker / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Aside from these patterns and comments from coworkers, have you ever heard voices or sounds that other people don’t seem to hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? I mean… not like a person standing there talking to me, if that’s what you mean. Sometimes at work the radio is so scratchy, and the buses make noises, brakes and doors and all that, and people act like you’re supposed to catch every word through all that static.\n\nSo I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed hearing things that nobody else hears, exactly. It’s more that the comments line up, and then you start paying attention, because if you miss something on dispatch they’ll make it seem like it’s your fault even when the system was slow that day.\n\nDoctor: If someone you really trusted showed you clear proof that dispatch and the other drivers weren’t actually coordinating or reporting on you, how likely is it that you would change your mind about this?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was someone I really trusted, I’d look at what they were showing me. I’m not trying to be difficult. But it would have to explain a lot, because it’s not just one thing. It’s the route changes showing up, then compliance acting like they already know, and then the little radio comments right after.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I’d just change my mind right away. People can show you one screen or one report, but that doesn’t mean certain drivers aren’t still reporting things. Sometimes the system looks clean on paper, you know? Like at work they’ll say everything is “procedure,” but then everyone knows there’s the real way things get handled.\n\nDoctor: Outside of what’s happening at work, do you have any other strong beliefs that you feel sure are true but that other people say are strange or hard to believe?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I mean… not really like that. It’s mostly the dispatch and compliance thing, because that’s where it’s happening. Like, I don’t walk around thinking random people at the grocery store are involved or anything.\n\nBut at work, yeah, I feel pretty sure. The computer system shows things too fast sometimes, like when I change a route or something gets adjusted, and then compliance is already acting like they know. And then some driver will make a comment over the radio that’s not direct, but it matches what just happened. People say it’s normal oversight, but I’ve been around it enough to know when drivers are reporting back. It’s not just me being sensitive.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like your thoughts, actions, or movements are being controlled or influenced by someone or something outside of you, rather than fully coming from you yourself?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Controlled like my arms or body? No, not exactly like that. I don’t feel like somebody is moving me around.

It’s more that at work, what I do on the route feels… watched, influenced, I guess. Not always direct, but enough that you can tell certain drivers are reporting back. People keep saying it’s just normal oversight, but it doesn’t feel normal when it keeps lining up like that.

Like at work they’ll say everything is “procedure,” but then everyone knows there’s the real way things get handled.\n\nDoctor: Outside of what’s happening at work, do you have any other strong beliefs that you feel sure are true but that other people say are strange or hard to believe?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I mean… not really like that. It’s mostly the dispatch and compliance thing, because that’s where it’s happening. Like, I don’t walk around thinking random people at the grocery store are involved or anything.\n\nBut at work, yeah, I feel pretty sure. The computer system shows things too fast sometimes, like when I change a route or something gets adjusted, and then compliance is already acting like they know. And then some driver will make a comment over the radio that’s not direct, but it matches what just happened. People say it’s normal oversight, but I’ve been around it enough to know when drivers are reporting back. It’s not just me being sensitive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts, actions, or movements are being controlled or influenced by someone or something outside of you, rather than fully coming from you yourself?\n\nPatient: Controlled like my arms or body? No, not exactly like that. I don’t feel like somebody is moving me around.\n\nIt’s more that at work, what I do on the route feels… watched, influenced, I guess. Like if I change something in the computer system, dispatch and compliance seem to know right away, and then the radio chatter starts up with these little comments. Not always direct, but enough that you can tell certain drivers are reporting back. People keep saying it’s just normal oversight, but it doesn’t feel normal when it keeps lining up like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel as if other people can somehow hear your thoughts or know exactly what you are thinking, even when you haven’t said anything out loud?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / transportation_worker / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system and little comments on the radio, and even when people say it’s just normal oversight, I can tell certain drivers are reporting on me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think they can hear my thoughts, like literally inside my head. It’s not like that.

But at work, it feels like they know what I’m going to do or what I just decided before I’ve even said anything. Like I’ll make a route change in the computer, or even be thinking through what makes sense for the route, and then dispatch or compliance is already acting like they’re on top of it. And then someone on the radio makes one of those little comments — not direct, but close enough that it feels like they know.

So… not thoughts exactly. More like they’re tracking it through the system and certain drivers are reporting back, and then everybody pretends it’s just normal oversight. That’s the part that bothers me.

Energized and not needing sleep for days… I don’t know. I’m usually tired if I don’t sleep. Sometimes I’ll stay up too late thinking about stuff, or I’ll get on my phone and then it’s suddenly late, but that’s not really the same as feeling great or super driven. 

So… no, not that I’ve noticed.

It’s… it’s been messing things up more than I want to admit. At work especially. I keep trying to double-check everything because I don’t want them twisting what I did, and then I end up falling behind on the route reports. And sometimes when I give instructions, I guess they come out confusing, because drivers complain that I’m not being clear.

My husband’s had to take over meals and phone calls and just basic planning stuff, like appointments or what we’re doing that day.

I mean, I still go to work, mostly, but it’s not like before. I don’t really relax much. Even when we watch something, I’m half paying attention, half thinking about work.

Sometimes at work the radio is so scratchy, and the buses make noises, brakes and doors and all that, and people act like you’re supposed to catch every word through all that static.\n\nSo I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed hearing things that nobody else hears, exactly. It’s more that the comments line up, and then you start paying attention, because if you miss something on dispatch they’ll make it seem like it’s your fault even when the system was slow that day.\n\nDoctor: If someone you really trusted showed you clear proof that dispatch and the other drivers weren’t actually coordinating or reporting on you, how likely is it that you would change your mind about this?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was someone I really trusted, I’d look at what they were showing me. I’m not trying to be difficult. But it would have to explain a lot, because it’s not just one thing. It’s the route changes showing up, then compliance acting like they already know, and then the little radio comments right after.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I’d just change my mind right away. People can show you one screen or one report, but that doesn’t mean certain drivers aren’t still reporting things. Sometimes the system looks clean on paper, you know? Like at work they’ll say everything is “procedure,” but then everyone knows there’s the real way things get handled.\n\nDoctor: Outside of what’s happening at work, do you have any other strong beliefs that you feel sure are true but that other people say are strange or hard to believe?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I mean… not really like that. It’s mostly the dispatch and compliance thing, because that’s where it’s happening. Like, I don’t walk around thinking random people at the grocery store are involved or anything.\n\nBut at work, yeah, I feel pretty sure. The computer system shows things too fast sometimes, like when I change a route or something gets adjusted, and then compliance is already acting like they know. And then some driver will make a comment over the radio that’s not direct, but it matches what just happened. People say it’s normal oversight, but I’ve been around it enough to know when drivers are reporting back. It’s not just me being sensitive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts, actions, or movements are being controlled or influenced by someone or something outside of you, rather than fully coming from you yourself?\n\nPatient: Controlled like my arms or body? No, not exactly like that. I don’t feel like somebody is moving me around.\n\nIt’s more that at work, what I do on the route feels… watched, influenced, I guess. Like if I change something in the computer system, dispatch and compliance seem to know right away, and then the radio chatter starts up with these little comments. Not always direct, but enough that you can tell certain drivers are reporting back. People keep saying it’s just normal oversight, but it doesn’t feel normal when it keeps lining up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as if other people can somehow hear your thoughts or know exactly what you are thinking, even when you haven’t said anything out loud?\n\nPatient: I don’t think they can hear my thoughts, like literally inside my head. It’s not like that.\n\nBut at work, it feels like they know what I’m going to do or what I just decided before I’ve even said anything. Like I’ll make a route change in the computer, or even be thinking through what makes sense for the route, and then dispatch or compliance is already acting like they’re on top of it. And then someone on the radio makes one of those little comments — not direct, but close enough that it feels like they know.\n\nSo… not thoughts exactly. More like they’re tracking it through the system and certain drivers are reporting back, and then everybody pretends it’s just normal oversight. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least several days when you felt unusually energized or upbeat, needed much less sleep than usual, and were more active or driven than is typical for you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve had times where I’m busy and I’ll push through, like if the schedule is messed up or there’s a lot going on, but that’s more just needing to get things done.\n\nEnergized and not needing sleep for days… I don’t know. I’m usually tired if I don’t sleep. Sometimes I’ll stay up too late thinking about stuff, or I’ll get on my phone and then it’s suddenly late, but that’s not really the same as feeling great or super driven. \n\nSo… no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: How much have these worries about dispatch and the other drivers been affecting your day-to-day life, for example your ability to work, relax, or enjoy things outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been messing things up more than I want to admit. At work especially. I keep trying to double-check everything because I don’t want them twisting what I did, and then I end up falling behind on the route reports. And sometimes when I give instructions, I guess they come out confusing, because drivers complain that I’m not being clear.\n\nAnd then at home, it follows me there too. Like I’ll be trying to make dinner and then I start thinking about what somebody said on the radio, or whether I missed something in the system, and suddenly I haven’t done anything. My husband’s had to take over meals and phone calls and just basic planning stuff, like appointments or what we’re doing that day.\n\nI mean, I still go to work, mostly, but it’s not like before. I don’t really relax much. Even when we watch something, I’m half paying attention, half thinking about work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I mean… maybe for like a second, if someone says, “That’s just normal oversight,” I’ll try to tell myself that. Like, okay, maybe compliance is just checking the system because that’s their job.

But then something happens again — I make a route change, or I’m thinking through one, and then there’s some little comment on the radio, or a driver acts like they already know what I did. And it’s not just one thing, it’s the pattern. That’s what people don’t get.

So I guess I’ve wondered if I’m reading too much into it, but it doesn’t last. Because it keeps lining up too neatly. Certain drivers are reporting back, and dispatch and compliance can see it through the computer, and then everyone acts like I’m supposed to believe it’s coincidence.

Sometimes at work the radio is so scratchy, and the buses make noises, brakes and doors and all that, and people act like you’re supposed to catch every word through all that static.\n\nSo I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed hearing things that nobody else hears, exactly. It’s more that the comments line up, and then you start paying attention, because if you miss something on dispatch they’ll make it seem like it’s your fault even when the system was slow that day.\n\nDoctor: If someone you really trusted showed you clear proof that dispatch and the other drivers weren’t actually coordinating or reporting on you, how likely is it that you would change your mind about this?\n\nPatient: I mean… if it was someone I really trusted, I’d look at what they were showing me. I’m not trying to be difficult. But it would have to explain a lot, because it’s not just one thing. It’s the route changes showing up, then compliance acting like they already know, and then the little radio comments right after.\n\nSo… I don’t know that I’d just change my mind right away. People can show you one screen or one report, but that doesn’t mean certain drivers aren’t still reporting things. Sometimes the system looks clean on paper, you know? Like at work they’ll say everything is “procedure,” but then everyone knows there’s the real way things get handled.\n\nDoctor: Outside of what’s happening at work, do you have any other strong beliefs that you feel sure are true but that other people say are strange or hard to believe?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? I mean… not really like that. It’s mostly the dispatch and compliance thing, because that’s where it’s happening. Like, I don’t walk around thinking random people at the grocery store are involved or anything.\n\nBut at work, yeah, I feel pretty sure. The computer system shows things too fast sometimes, like when I change a route or something gets adjusted, and then compliance is already acting like they know. And then some driver will make a comment over the radio that’s not direct, but it matches what just happened. People say it’s normal oversight, but I’ve been around it enough to know when drivers are reporting back. It’s not just me being sensitive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like your thoughts, actions, or movements are being controlled or influenced by someone or something outside of you, rather than fully coming from you yourself?\n\nPatient: Controlled like my arms or body? No, not exactly like that. I don’t feel like somebody is moving me around.\n\nIt’s more that at work, what I do on the route feels… watched, influenced, I guess. Like if I change something in the computer system, dispatch and compliance seem to know right away, and then the radio chatter starts up with these little comments. Not always direct, but enough that you can tell certain drivers are reporting back. People keep saying it’s just normal oversight, but it doesn’t feel normal when it keeps lining up like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel as if other people can somehow hear your thoughts or know exactly what you are thinking, even when you haven’t said anything out loud?\n\nPatient: I don’t think they can hear my thoughts, like literally inside my head. It’s not like that.\n\nBut at work, it feels like they know what I’m going to do or what I just decided before I’ve even said anything. Like I’ll make a route change in the computer, or even be thinking through what makes sense for the route, and then dispatch or compliance is already acting like they’re on top of it. And then someone on the radio makes one of those little comments — not direct, but close enough that it feels like they know.\n\nSo… not thoughts exactly. More like they’re tracking it through the system and certain drivers are reporting back, and then everybody pretends it’s just normal oversight. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least several days when you felt unusually energized or upbeat, needed much less sleep than usual, and were more active or driven than is typical for you?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’ve had times where I’m busy and I’ll push through, like if the schedule is messed up or there’s a lot going on, but that’s more just needing to get things done.\n\nEnergized and not needing sleep for days… I don’t know. I’m usually tired if I don’t sleep. Sometimes I’ll stay up too late thinking about stuff, or I’ll get on my phone and then it’s suddenly late, but that’s not really the same as feeling great or super driven. \n\nSo… no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: How much have these worries about dispatch and the other drivers been affecting your day-to-day life, for example your ability to work, relax, or enjoy things outside of work?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s been messing things up more than I want to admit. At work especially. I keep trying to double-check everything because I don’t want them twisting what I did, and then I end up falling behind on the route reports. And sometimes when I give instructions, I guess they come out confusing, because drivers complain that I’m not being clear.\n\nAnd then at home, it follows me there too. Like I’ll be trying to make dinner and then I start thinking about what somebody said on the radio, or whether I missed something in the system, and suddenly I haven’t done anything. My husband’s had to take over meals and phone calls and just basic planning stuff, like appointments or what we’re doing that day.\n\nI mean, I still go to work, mostly, but it’s not like before. I don’t really relax much. Even when we watch something, I’m half paying attention, half thinking about work.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been a moment, even briefly, when you thought to yourself, \"Maybe I’m wrong about them coordinating and reporting on me\"?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe for like a second, if someone says, “That’s just normal oversight,” I’ll try to tell myself that. Like, okay, maybe compliance is just checking the system because that’s their job.\n\nBut then something happens again — I make a route change, or I’m thinking through one, and then there’s some little comment on the radio, or a driver acts like they already know what I did. And it’s not just one thing, it’s the pattern. That’s what people don’t get.\n\nSo I guess I’ve wondered if I’m reading too much into it, but it doesn’t last. Because it keeps lining up too neatly. Certain drivers are reporting back, and dispatch and compliance can see it through the computer, and then everyone acts like I’m supposed to believe it’s coincidence.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nControlled like my arms or body? No, not exactly like that. I don’t feel like somebody is moving me around.\n\nIt’s more that at work, what I do on the route feels… watched, influenced, I guess. Like if I change something in the computer system, dispatch and compliance seem to know right away, and then the radio chatter starts up with these little comments. Not always direct, but enough that you can tell certain drivers are reporting back. People keep saying it’s just normal oversight, but it doesn’t feel normal when it keeps lining up like that.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.
